Anthropic is&nbsp;challenging the Pentagon for designating it&nbsp;a supply chain risk after it&nbsp;clashed with the department over how to&nbsp;use A.I. in&nbsp;warfare.</span></p><p><span>The participation of&nbsp;Google employees in&nbsp;the legal briefing added to&nbsp;some officials’ concerns about the company, reminding decision makers in&nbsp;the Pentagon and elsewhere of&nbsp;the past protests against the use of&nbsp;A.I. in&nbsp;weapons, one former official with knowledge of&nbsp;the discussions said. Some Trump administration officials are worried that even if&nbsp;Google agrees to&nbsp;have its A.I. used widely, the company could bow out again, the former official&nbsp;added.</span></p></div></details><p><span>In&nbsp;this time, I&nbsp;sent several memos to&nbsp;Google Legal suggesting that Google file an&nbsp;amicus too. I&nbsp;reminded them that if&nbsp;Google let the threat stand against Anthropic, the government would now have a&nbsp;gun to&nbsp;point at&nbsp;Google in&nbsp;all future negotiations: comply, or&nbsp;be labeled a&nbsp;supply-chain risk. In&nbsp;the end, Google didn’t&nbsp;file an&nbsp;amicus (but </span><a href="https://www.datacenterdynamics.com/en/news/microsoft-files-amicus-brief-in-support-of-anthropics-dod-lawsuit/"><span>Microsoft did</span></a><span>).</span></p><h1><span>Senior management insisted Google wouldn’t&nbsp;cave</span></h1><p><i><span>All through&nbsp;March</span></i></p><p><span>As&nbsp;I talked more with senior management, they kept arguing something like: “don’t&nbsp;worry. Leadership cares about these issues, too. They won’t&nbsp;sign an&nbsp;‘all lawful use’ deal.” I&nbsp;considered the idea. Might it be&nbsp;true?</span></p><p><span>“No, it’s&nbsp;not true,” I&nbsp;concluded: not unless someone </span><i><span>forced</span></i><span> leadership to&nbsp;hold the line. I&nbsp;wrote a&nbsp;citation-heavy memo explaining why Google was likely to&nbsp;cave. I&nbsp;pointed to&nbsp;Google’s&nbsp;history of&nbsp;complying in&nbsp;lower-stakes situations (like </span><a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/04/google-broke-its-promise-me-now-ice-has-my-data"><span>voluntarily handing over a&nbsp;student protester’s&nbsp;information to&nbsp;ICE</span></a><span>); I&nbsp;pointed to&nbsp;Google’s&nbsp;large share of&nbsp;government contracts; and I&nbsp;pointed to&nbsp;how Google DeepMind’s&nbsp;AI principles had </span><a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/technology/2025/02/04/google-ai-policies-weapons-harm/"><span>disintegrated into noncommittal vagueness</span></a><span>. I&nbsp;reviewed some of&nbsp;the pressures confronting Google, including Google’s&nbsp;mixed anti-monopoly exposure and </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/interactive/2026/us/trump-administration-lawsuits.html"><span>the historically poor track record of&nbsp;the current Department of&nbsp;Justice.</span></a></p><p><span>I&nbsp;shared this memo quite a&nbsp;few times. As&nbsp;best I&nbsp;can tell, I&nbsp;wasn’t&nbsp;able to&nbsp;persuade these senior&nbsp;employees.</span></p><h1><span>Preparing for lunch with Jeff&nbsp;Dean</span></h1><p><i><span>March 10th, 2026</span></i></p><p><span>I&nbsp;stayed focused on&nbsp;my goal. I&nbsp;needed to&nbsp;move Sundar. Jeff could, I&nbsp;estimated, move Sundar. Did Jeff </span><i><span>want</span></i><span> to&nbsp;move Sundar? Would he&nbsp;walk from a&nbsp;company where he&nbsp;is revered, with a&nbsp;prestigious job seemingly tailored to&nbsp;him, leaving behind a&nbsp;quarter-lifetime of&nbsp;memories? On&nbsp;the other hand, every tech company in&nbsp;the world would want to&nbsp;hire Jeff, his legacy seemed secure, he&nbsp;already had who-knows-how-much money, and leaving would show&nbsp;principle.</span></p><p><span>I&nbsp;didn’t&nbsp;want to&nbsp;manipulate Jeff. I&nbsp;wanted to&nbsp;be real with him as&nbsp;one concerned person to&nbsp;another. I&nbsp;even avoided reading more than one article about him to&nbsp;keep myself from subconsciously tailoring my&nbsp;arguments to&nbsp;appeal to his&nbsp;psyche.</span></p><p><span>The way I&nbsp;saw it&nbsp;was: I&nbsp;don’t&nbsp;need to&nbsp;get Jeff to&nbsp;agree to&nbsp;quit over this. In&nbsp;a sense, he’d&nbsp;</span><i><span>already</span></i><span> agreed to&nbsp;quit over it. He&nbsp;had signed a&nbsp;pledge in&nbsp;2018:</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://futureoflife.org/open-letter/lethal-autonomous-weapons-pledge/"><b><span>Lethal Autonomous Weapons Pledge</span></b></a><span> (signed by&nbsp;5,218&nbsp;people)</span></p><p><span>The decision to&nbsp;take a&nbsp;human life should never be&nbsp;delegated to&nbsp;a machine. There is&nbsp;a moral component to&nbsp;this position, that we&nbsp;should not allow machines to&nbsp;make life-taking decisions for which others—or nobody—will be&nbsp;culpable. There is&nbsp;also a&nbsp;powerful pragmatic argument: lethal autonomous weapons, selecting and engaging targets without human intervention, would be&nbsp;dangerously destabilizing for every country and individual. […]</span></p><p><span>By&nbsp;removing the risk, attributability, and difficulty of&nbsp;taking human lives, lethal autonomous weapons could become powerful instruments of&nbsp;violence and oppression, especially when linked to&nbsp;surveillance and data systems. […]</span></p><p><span>We, the undersigned, call upon governments and government leaders to&nbsp;create a&nbsp;future with strong international norms, regulations and laws against lethal autonomous weapons. These currently being absent, we&nbsp;opt to&nbsp;hold ourselves to&nbsp;a high standard: we&nbsp;will neither participate in&nbsp;nor support the development, manufacture, trade, or&nbsp;use of&nbsp;lethal autonomous&nbsp;weapons.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Signers include: Google DeepMind (the organization), Demis Hassabis, Shane Legg (cofounder, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shane_Legg"><span>now Chief AGI Scientist</span></a><span>), Raia Hadsell (</span><a href="https://raiahadsell.com/index.html"><span>VP of&nbsp;Research</span></a><span>), Jay Yagnik (</span><a href="https://research.google/people/author36197/?&amp;type=google"><span>VP and Engineering Fellow at&nbsp;Google, leading large parts of&nbsp;Google AI</span></a><span>) and 🥁🥁🥁 Jeff&nbsp;Dean:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 65.73033707865169%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784069971/lexical_client_uploads/lytu6q4m1ocxp3txluc9.png" /><figcaption><p><br /><a href="https://xcancel.com/JeffDean/status/2026566490619879574"><span>Jeff Dean’s&nbsp;quote-tweet</span></a><span> · </span><a href="https://xcancel.com/TopherSpiro/status/2026668689802547231"><span>Topher Spiro’s&nbsp;tweet</span></a><span> · </span><a href="https://xcancel.com/JeffDean/status/2026683499919610153"><span>Jeff Dean’s&nbsp;reply</span></a><span>. Notice: Jeff freely reiterated his pledge and agreed that “AI&nbsp;for mass surveillance of&nbsp;Americans” is&nbsp;“the last thing [he&nbsp;wants].”</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>I&nbsp;reasoned that if&nbsp;Jeff “will neither participate in… nor support the development… or&nbsp;use of&nbsp;lethal autonomous weapons,” then logically, </span><i><span>Jeff would have to&nbsp;quit Google if&nbsp;it signed an&nbsp;“all lawful use” deal.</span></i><span> I&nbsp;didn’t&nbsp;know if&nbsp;Jeff was </span><i><span>actually</span></i><span> ready to&nbsp;do anything like that. I&nbsp;wanted to&nbsp;prepare for the possibility.</span></p><h2><span>I&nbsp;arranged social&nbsp;support</span></h2><p><span>Jeff, like all of&nbsp;us, is&nbsp;a human being. Humans tend to&nbsp;be worried by&nbsp;acting alone against a&nbsp;powerful entity. Even though I&nbsp;imagined Jeff’s&nbsp;weight would be&nbsp;enough to&nbsp;move Google on&nbsp;its own, I&nbsp;wanted him to&nbsp;feel supported. The first part of&nbsp;that was the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Jeff_Dean__you_re_our_only_hope" rel="noreferrer"><span>petition I&nbsp;organized</span></a><span>, signed by&nbsp;over 250 GDM employees asking Jeff to&nbsp;</span><i><span>fight</span></i><span> for them. The second part, though, was&nbsp;quieter.</span></p><p><span>I&nbsp;secured backup from several senior Google employees. If&nbsp;Jeff would agree to&nbsp;put his foot down with Sundar, they signaled that they would too. That said, the intended coalition was of&nbsp;size</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefunv7cpolnm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnunv7cpolnm">[13]</a></sup></span><span> one: Jeff. I&nbsp;expected that Jeff’s&nbsp;influence would be&nbsp;enough on&nbsp;its own, as&nbsp;his departure could leave the Gemini project in&nbsp;shambles. Maybe Google would care more about its AI&nbsp;program than about retaliation from&nbsp;Trump.</span></p><p><span>I&nbsp;guessed that Sundar was skilled at&nbsp;defusing pressure from employees. I&nbsp;hoped this was a&nbsp;gambit he&nbsp;wouldn’t&nbsp;be able to&nbsp;stop, even if&nbsp;he knew about&nbsp;it.</span></p><h2><span>The art of the&nbsp;deal</span></h2><p><i><span>March 13th, 2026</span></i></p><p><span>Was Jeff a&nbsp;wrestler? I&nbsp;read an&nbsp;account of&nbsp;Demis </span><a href="https://colossus.com/article/project-mario-demis-hassabis-deepmind-mallaby/"><span>trying to&nbsp;negotiate with Sundar for a&nbsp;separate legal structure for DeepMind</span></a><span>. Any deal with Sundar seemed like it&nbsp;would need wrestling. </span><i><span>Persistent</span></i><span> wrestling. Furthermore, Sundar might try to&nbsp;defuse the tension with vague promises, so&nbsp;Jeff would need a specific&nbsp;proposal.</span></p><p><span>I&nbsp;mean, imagine Jeff and I&nbsp;sit down for lunch. Imagine he’s&nbsp;on board. Imagine he&nbsp;goes to&nbsp;Sundar and says “If&nbsp;we sign this deal, I&nbsp;won’t&nbsp;be able to&nbsp;stay at&nbsp;Google. Help me&nbsp;stay.” Imagine Sundar says “You’re&nbsp;important to&nbsp;me, Jeff. What would it&nbsp;take?”. What does Jeff say? “Don’t&nbsp;sign a&nbsp;bad deal” would probably lead to&nbsp;a bad contract that looks less bad on the&nbsp;surface.</span></p><h3><span>Criteria for a&nbsp;contract</span></h3><p><span>I&nbsp;needed to&nbsp;draft contract language which would actually&nbsp;work.</span></p><p><b><span>Good red lines:</span></b><span> Rule out the questionable use cases (autonomous targeting without human control, untargeted profiling) while allowing trustworthy ones like missile defense. Avoid the weaknesses flagged in&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.lawfaremedia.org/article/the-situation--thinking-about-anthropic-s-red-lines"><span>legal analysis of&nbsp;Anthropic’s&nbsp;red lines</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>Robust red lines:</span></b><span> Cloud would push deals through any loophole, Google Legal seemed unlikely to&nbsp;tighten my&nbsp;drafting, and the Pentagon wouldn’t&nbsp;want terms at&nbsp;all. The language had to&nbsp;hold under pressure, with auditing that respected classification and operational&nbsp;security.</span></p><p><b><span>Minimal trust assumptions:</span></b><span> I&nbsp;made the Chief Scientist </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Certificate_authority"><span>the single root of&nbsp;trust that everything else hangs off of</span></a><span>&nbsp;(I&nbsp;was betting everything on&nbsp;Jeff anyway). The Chief Scientist would staff a&nbsp;Review Body to&nbsp;advise on&nbsp;contracts.</span></p><p><b><span>Accountability via transparency:</span></b><span> The Review Body would only privately advise Jeff and Sundar, but </span><i><span>overriding</span></i><span> it&nbsp;surfaces in&nbsp;a yearly transparency report to&nbsp;all AI&nbsp;employees. Dissolving it&nbsp;would require advance notice and disclosure of&nbsp;the exact outstanding non-compliance findings. I&nbsp;worked to&nbsp;ensure the Body couldn’t&nbsp;be defanged as&nbsp;quietly as&nbsp;Google’s&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.cnbc.com/2025/02/04/google-removes-pledge-to-not-use-ai-for-weapons-surveillance.html"><span>2018 principles were</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>Minimal pain to&nbsp;opposed stakeholders:</span></b><span> I&nbsp;gave Cloud 2 of&nbsp;7 seats, recused staff only from their own deals, capped delays at&nbsp;10 days, and protected deliberations under attorney-client&nbsp;privilege.</span></p><p><span>I&nbsp;considered a&nbsp;negotiations round. The counterparties would object to&nbsp;</span><i><span>something</span></i><span>, and I&nbsp;didn’t&nbsp;want that </span><i><span>something</span></i><span> to&nbsp;be load-bearing. Therefore, I&nbsp;included less-important provisions meant to&nbsp;be negotiated away, like “the Review Body can escalate to&nbsp;Alphabet’s&nbsp;Board via supermajority&nbsp;vote.”</span></p><h3><span>The Framework I&nbsp;created</span></h3><p><span>In&nbsp;my personal time (taking vacation days), I&nbsp;created language that would be&nbsp;a good starting point. I&nbsp;composed 25 pages’ worth of&nbsp;“good starting point” material. I&nbsp;have published the Framework on&nbsp;my site in&nbsp;generic form applicable to&nbsp;any provider of&nbsp;both cloud and&nbsp;AI.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>Excerpts from </span></b><a href="https://turntrout.com/military-ai"><b><span>A Red Line and Oversight Framework for Military AI</span></b></a><span>&nbsp;</span></p><p><i><span>I&nbsp;sometimes call this “the Framework” as&nbsp;shorthand.</span></i></p><p><span>This Framework proposes two narrow Standards for the AI&nbsp;provided by&nbsp;The Company to&nbsp;government entities exercising coercive&nbsp;authority:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Human control over targeting and use of&nbsp;force.</span></b><span> The Company’s&nbsp;AI won’t&nbsp;be used in&nbsp;systems that select and engage targets for force without appropriate human control over each engagement, evaluated on&nbsp;a use-case-by-use-case basis. Applies whether The Company provides the targeting system directly or&nbsp;simply provides AI&nbsp;components in&nbsp;a targeting pipeline. Includes a&nbsp;right to&nbsp;legal transparency regarding how systems will be&nbsp;lawfully deployed, with compliance verification conducted by&nbsp;a mutually agreed neutral auditor. Does not restrict anti-munition defensive systems, intelligence analysis subject to&nbsp;Standard 2, logistics, or&nbsp;R&amp;D.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>No&nbsp;untargeted AI&nbsp;profiling.</span></b><span> The Company’s&nbsp;AI won’t&nbsp;convert bulk data into individualized intelligence on&nbsp;people who aren’t&nbsp;already specific, identified subjects of&nbsp;investigation. For all persons regardless of&nbsp;nationality, individualized AI-assisted analysis must be&nbsp;proportionate to&nbsp;the security interest served, may not be&nbsp;initiated based solely on&nbsp;demographic characteristics or&nbsp;political expression, and AI-generated outputs may not serve as&nbsp;the sole basis for initiating individualized scrutiny. Heightened protections for all persons in&nbsp;the U.S. regardless of&nbsp;status. Permits targeted analysis of&nbsp;identified subjects, aggregate research, and conflict zone analysis that improves noncombatant protection.</span></li></ol><p><b><span>Transparency via yearly internal reports:</span></b><span> An&nbsp;advisory seven-person Defense AI&nbsp;Review Body of&nbsp;senior staff, appointed by&nbsp;and reporting to&nbsp;the Chief&nbsp;Scientist.</span></p><p><b><span>Superseded by&nbsp;future laws:</span></b><span> If&nbsp;Congress passes substantial legislation governing these usages, the Chief Scientist and Review Body (by&nbsp;supermajority vote) can retire one or both&nbsp;Standards.</span></p><p><b><span>The Company will not accept “all lawful use” as&nbsp;its standard.</span></b><span> Where “all lawful use” language is&nbsp;demanded, The Company will require access to&nbsp;the legal memoranda establishing the lawfulness of&nbsp;intended uses. That transparency gives The Company the information to&nbsp;evaluate each use case against its Standards. Some will meet The Company’s&nbsp;standard. Some won’t. The ones that don’t, The Company&nbsp;declines.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>With the help of&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/tom-smith"><code><span>TomSmith</span></code></a><span>, I&nbsp;got feedback from experts in&nbsp;military and surveillance law. In&nbsp;particular, I&nbsp;got feedback from a&nbsp;foremost legal expert on&nbsp;human/AI warfighting integration. They said my&nbsp;Framework was “actually pretty good” :) and suggested improvements.</span></p><h1><span>My&nbsp;lunch with&nbsp;Jeff</span></h1><p><i><span>March 17th, 2026</span></i></p><img alt="Aerial view of the Google Gradient Canopy building in Mountain View, characterized by its distinctive, large-scale dragon-scale roof structure, surrounded by roads, trees, and walkways." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iKm2FhpWkuuBojm82/dhbxtmsvmt8qmfvbinmx" /><p><span>A&nbsp;friend drove me&nbsp;down to&nbsp;Mountain View to&nbsp;meet Jeff. Wearing a&nbsp;crisp dress shirt and slacks, I&nbsp;walked through the Gradient Canopy office. I&nbsp;had the Framework in&nbsp;my bag and I&nbsp;had a&nbsp;question on&nbsp;my mind: “What, if&nbsp;anything, does Jeff want to&nbsp;do?”. Was I&nbsp;going to&nbsp;meet a&nbsp;crusader? A&nbsp;bystander? A&nbsp;strategist?</span></p><p><span>A&nbsp;month prior, I&nbsp;asked for this lunch so&nbsp;we could discuss “constructive options” for making Google’s&nbsp;contract situation more ethical. Would he&nbsp;have plans of&nbsp;his own? Surely he&nbsp;could. He&nbsp;had so&nbsp;much more visibility and experience. I&nbsp;readied myself to&nbsp;toss my&nbsp;Framework aside and follow a&nbsp;better plan devised using more information. I&nbsp;didn’t&nbsp;care much. Even if&nbsp;Sundar adopted my&nbsp;Framework, I&nbsp;knew I&nbsp;was unlikely to&nbsp;be credited. That made me&nbsp;a bit sad, but I&nbsp;wanted to&nbsp;get the job&nbsp;done.</span></p><p><span>I&nbsp;had lunch with Jeff under the California sun. I&nbsp;proposed that Jeff head the potential Review Body, but he&nbsp;didn’t&nbsp;take up&nbsp;the idea. Beyond that, I&nbsp;won’t&nbsp;discuss the details of&nbsp;our lunch. I&nbsp;can only point to&nbsp;his public conduct: He&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Preparing_for_lunch_with_Jeff_Dean" rel="noreferrer"><span>tweeted</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Jeff_signs_an_amicus_brief_supporting_Anthropic" rel="noreferrer"><span>signed an&nbsp;amicus brief in&nbsp;support of&nbsp;Anthropic</span></a><span>. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Google_quietly_signs_the_deal" rel="noreferrer"><span>Google later signed the deal</span></a><span>. Jeff is&nbsp;still at&nbsp;Google, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#how-can-a-pledge-signer-remain-at-gdm"><span>despite his pledge</span></a><span>.</span></p><h2><span>Searching for another path to&nbsp;impact</span></h2><p><span>I&nbsp;had expected this—I had simply judged the odds good enough to&nbsp;justify the shot. Without Jeff championing the Review Body, the chances of&nbsp;success dropped a&nbsp;lot. I&nbsp;still thought the large possible benefits were worth continued effort on&nbsp;my part. The other obvious escalation pathway was to Demis&nbsp;Hassabis.</span></p><p><span>In&nbsp;2014, </span><a href="https://time.com/7013685/google-ai-deepmind-military-contracts-israel/"><span>Google acquired DeepMind on&nbsp;an explicit promise: its AI&nbsp;would never be&nbsp;used for military or&nbsp;weapons purposes</span></a><span>. In&nbsp;2018, all of&nbsp;DeepMind’s&nbsp;co-founders (including Demis) signed </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#preparing-for-lunch-with-jeff-dean"><span>the FLI lethal autonomous weapons pledge</span></a><span>, as&nbsp;did Google DeepMind </span><i><span>as an&nbsp;organization</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><a href="https://colossus.com/article/project-mario-demis-hassabis-deepmind-mallaby/"><span>Demis had already fought Sundar to&nbsp;ensure a&nbsp;future DeepMind AGI wouldn’t&nbsp;be chained to&nbsp;Google’s&nbsp;profit incentives (though he&nbsp;lost in&nbsp;the end).</span></a><span> I&nbsp;thought, “Demis won’t&nbsp;quit over this. But if&nbsp;he pushes, he&nbsp;might get some of&nbsp;the transparency mechanisms.” I&nbsp;looked for a&nbsp;non-awkward way to&nbsp;get my&nbsp;proposal in&nbsp;front of&nbsp;Demis. Jeff could have sent it&nbsp;no problem, but I&nbsp;had to look&nbsp;elsewhere.</span></p><p><span>I&nbsp;looked. I&nbsp;did learn that there was one senior person taking real initiative and working hard to&nbsp;stop the deal. I&nbsp;appreciated that. But I&nbsp;only knew of&nbsp;one.</span></p><p><span>In&nbsp;any case, after a&nbsp;week of&nbsp;trying to&nbsp;escalate through management chains, I&nbsp;decided to&nbsp;just do&nbsp;the awkward way&nbsp;instead.</span></p><h1><span>No&nbsp;one is&nbsp;responding, so&nbsp;why not just DM the&nbsp;CEO?</span></h1><p><i><span>April 1st, 2026</span></i></p><p><span>After all, what’s&nbsp;the worst that would realistically happen? In&nbsp;my estimation, he&nbsp;might say “not appropriate, send through your manager next time.” Whatever.</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>My&nbsp;message to&nbsp;Demis</span></b></p><p><span>Demis, I&nbsp;drafted a&nbsp;Framework for military AI&nbsp;oversight at&nbsp;Google, along with two candidate standards (which can be&nbsp;considered separately). A&nbsp;foremost legal expert on&nbsp;human/AI warfighting integration said the Framework is&nbsp;“actually pretty good.” The oversight is&nbsp;advisory, providing independent technical and ethical assessment of&nbsp;defense AI&nbsp;deployments.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Demis told me&nbsp;to get the Framework evaluated by&nbsp;two senior people working in&nbsp;GDM policy. I&nbsp;sent it&nbsp;to them. They left the message on&nbsp;read.</span></p><h2><span>My&nbsp;Framework goes unevaluated</span></h2><p><span>Busy guys being busy, perhaps. A&nbsp;few days later, I&nbsp;pinged them. One sent me&nbsp;to talk to&nbsp;some of&nbsp;their reports. About a&nbsp;week passed, then I&nbsp;spoke with those reports. They seemed excited about the Framework but said that I&nbsp;wouldn’t&nbsp;hear back for months, which didn’t&nbsp;fit the time&nbsp;pressure.</span></p><p><span>I&nbsp;returned to&nbsp;the senior policy people to&nbsp;talk about next steps. One wanted me&nbsp;to circle back to&nbsp;them with updates as&nbsp;the situation developed. A&nbsp;few days later, I&nbsp;gave gentle pushback. I&nbsp;explained the unknown but potentially short timeframe we&nbsp;confronted, pointing to&nbsp;the Pentagon’s&nbsp;</span><a href="https://media.defense.gov/2026/Jan/12/2003855671/-1/-1/0/artificial-intelligence-strategy-for-the-department-of-war.pdf"><span>January 9th memo</span></a><span> giving ALL AI&nbsp;contractors a&nbsp;180-day deadline (falling on&nbsp;July 8th) to&nbsp;accept “any lawful use” contracts.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6i1k26tf8k"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6i1k26tf8k">[14]</a></sup></span><span> I&nbsp;basically said, “it’s&nbsp;fine if&nbsp;you think Demis shouldn’t&nbsp;look at&nbsp;the Framework again, but I&nbsp;want you to&nbsp;make that determination either way.” I&nbsp;even offered to&nbsp;fly from San Francisco to&nbsp;London just to&nbsp;answer any questions they had about the&nbsp;Framework.</span></p><p><span>They left the message on&nbsp;read.</span></p><p><span>I&nbsp;still don’t&nbsp;know what happened. The company’s&nbsp;CEO wanted it&nbsp;evaluated, after all. I&nbsp;didn’t&nbsp;expect them to&nbsp;loop </span><i><span>me</span></i><span> in, but I&nbsp;expected a&nbsp;“thanks, I’ll&nbsp;let Demis know my&nbsp;assessment.” It’s&nbsp;possible there was some unknown but legitimate complication. In&nbsp;any case, I’m&nbsp;not happy with this&nbsp;process.</span></p><h1><span>Google quietly signs the&nbsp;deal</span></h1><p><i><span>As&nbsp;</span></i><a href="https://www.theinformation.com/articles/google-signs-classified-ai-deal-pentagon-amid-employee-opposition"><i><span>reported</span></i></a><i><span> on&nbsp;April 27th, 2026</span></i></p><p><span>That weekend prior, I&nbsp;had heard rumblings. </span><a href="https://www.theverge.com/ai-artificial-intelligence/919326/google-ai-pentagon-classified-letter"><span>Along with over 600 other employees</span></a><span>, I&nbsp;signed a&nbsp;letter asking Sundar to&nbsp;say “no” to&nbsp;classified AI&nbsp;contracts.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflrvmfqx4duf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlrvmfqx4duf">[15]</a></sup></span><span> I&nbsp;asked Jeff if&nbsp;there was anything I&nbsp;could do&nbsp;on an&nbsp;informal basis, crossing reporting lines and bureaucracy to&nbsp;help him get </span><i><span>anything</span></i><span> done that he&nbsp;wanted. I’d&nbsp;work through the weekend, no&nbsp;problem, on&nbsp;whatever he&nbsp;thought was wise. Alas.</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://www.reuters.com/technology/google-signs-classified-ai-deal-with-pentagon-information-reports-2026-04-28/"><b><span>Google signs classified AI&nbsp;deal with&nbsp;Pentagon</span></b></a></p><p><span>The agreement allows the Pentagon to&nbsp;use Google’s&nbsp;AI for “any lawful government purpose.”</span></p><p><span>Google’s&nbsp;agreement requires it&nbsp;to help in&nbsp;adjusting the company’s&nbsp;AI safety settings and filters at&nbsp;the government’s&nbsp;request…</span></p><p><span>The contract includes language stating, “the parties agree that the AI&nbsp;System is&nbsp;not intended for, and should not be&nbsp;used for, domestic mass surveillance or&nbsp;autonomous weapons (including target selection) without appropriate human oversight and&nbsp;control.”</span></p><p><span>However, the agreement also says it&nbsp;does not give Google the right to&nbsp;control or&nbsp;veto lawful government operational decision-making.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>I&nbsp;found out at&nbsp;11:45 PM&nbsp;via a&nbsp;Signal group. Google never announced the deal internally. What surprised me&nbsp;was not that Google signed, but that the deal paid the barest of&nbsp;lip service to&nbsp;ethical concerns: the “should not” language </span><a href="https://x.com/CharlieBull0ck/status/2049249853947945369"><span>is not binding</span></a><span>.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 88.03719696321515%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1784080070/lexical_client_uploads/fn5auyqkzw63fihzpvsj.png" /><figcaption><p><br /><a href="https://xcancel.com/Turn_Trout/status/2049153749743264231"><span>My&nbsp;tweet</span></a><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>I&nbsp;went to&nbsp;the field’s&nbsp;premier safety &amp; ethics organization (IASEAI). I&nbsp;asked some of&nbsp;the most distinguished AI&nbsp;scientists (Bengio and Stuart). I&nbsp;built a&nbsp;coalition and a&nbsp;plan for Google’s&nbsp;most outspoken executive (Jeff). I&nbsp;even cold-messaged the CEO of&nbsp;my company (Demis), whose lieutenants never evaluated the proposal. Besides Jeff, none took </span><i><span>any</span></i><span> visible action to&nbsp;stop the deal. And the deal contains no&nbsp;binding provisions, which is&nbsp;what I’d&nbsp;expect if&nbsp;Jeff never threatened to&nbsp;walk.</span></p><p><span>The deal was&nbsp;inked.</span></p><hr /><h2><span>But also, Google stopped bidding for a drone contract?</span></h2><blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-04-28/google-drops-out-of-pentagon-drone-swarm-contest-after-advancing"><b><span>Google Drops Out of&nbsp;Pentagon Drone Swarm Contest After Advancing</span></b></a></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span>Google abruptly dropped out of&nbsp;a $100 million Pentagon prize challenge to&nbsp;create technology for voice-controlled, autonomous drone swarms after it&nbsp;was among the successful submissions, according to&nbsp;people briefed on the&nbsp;matter.</span></p><p><span>The company notified the government it&nbsp;wouldn’t&nbsp;participate further in&nbsp;the initiative, which seeks to&nbsp;create the technology needed to&nbsp;control drone swarms, on&nbsp;Feb. 11—a few weeks after the proposal was submitted, according to&nbsp;another person briefed on&nbsp;the matter. The decision followed an&nbsp;internal ethics review, according to&nbsp;records referencing it&nbsp;that were reviewed by&nbsp;Bloomberg News. Alphabet Inc.’s&nbsp;Google officially cited a&nbsp;lack of&nbsp;“resourcing” when it&nbsp;pulled out of&nbsp;the contest, according to the&nbsp;records.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Apparently, Google </span><i><span>does</span></i><span> have an&nbsp;ethics review with teeth that bite, at&nbsp;least sometimes, on&nbsp;</span><i><span>some</span></i><span> projects. That’s&nbsp;good.</span></p><p><span>However, this doesn’t&nbsp;exonerate Google’s&nbsp;decision to&nbsp;sign the deal. Let’s&nbsp;charitably suppose that Google has a&nbsp;process which </span><i><span>always</span></i><span> blocks contracts which will obviously be&nbsp;used for weapons in&nbsp;particular.</span></p><p><span>As&nbsp;I’ll&nbsp;soon cover, Google’s&nbsp;criterion is&nbsp;apparently that “the benefits substantially outweigh the harms.” How can Google weigh that if&nbsp;Google can’t&nbsp;know what “lawful purposes” Gemini will be&nbsp;used for? Under an&nbsp;“all lawful use” deal, </span><i><span>there will no&nbsp;longer be&nbsp;any warnings which would make ethics-minded employees like Jeff or&nbsp;Demis uncomfortable.</span></i></p><p><span>The government isn’t&nbsp;going to&nbsp;call Google and say “we&nbsp;used Gemini in&nbsp;a kill chain to&nbsp;bomb a&nbsp;bunch of&nbsp;people.” Similarly, the CEO of&nbsp;Anthropic </span><a href="https://www.forbes.com/sites/antoniopequenoiv/2026/06/10/anthropic-ceo-we-dont-know-exactly-how-claude-ai-was-used-in-iran-school-strike/"><span>still doesn’t&nbsp;know</span></a><span> what role, if&nbsp;any, Claude played in&nbsp;the bombing of&nbsp;an Iranian girls’ school. Even Anthropic doesn’t&nbsp;know, and Anthropic has publicly demonstrated far more appetite for contract transparency than Google&nbsp;has.</span></p><h1><span>Demis insists Google’s&nbsp;AI principles “haven’t&nbsp;changed”</span></h1><blockquote><p><a href="https://time.com/7280740/demis-hassabis-interview/"><b><span>Google DeepMind CEO Demis Hassabis on&nbsp;AI in&nbsp;the Military and What AGI Could Mean for&nbsp;Humanity</span></b></a></p><p><b><span>Interviewer:</span></b><span> When Google acquired DeepMind in&nbsp;2014 you signed a&nbsp;contract that said Google wouldn’t&nbsp;use your technology for military purposes. Since then, you’ve&nbsp;restructured. Now DeepMind tech is&nbsp;sold to&nbsp;various militaries, including the U.S. and Israel. You’ve&nbsp;talked about the huge upside of&nbsp;developing AGI.&nbsp;Do you feel like you compromised on&nbsp;that front in&nbsp;order to&nbsp;have the opportunity to&nbsp;make that technology?</span></p><p><b><span>Demis:</span></b><span> No, I&nbsp;don’t&nbsp;think so. I&nbsp;think we’ve&nbsp;updated things recently to&nbsp;partly take into account the much bigger geopolitical uncertainties we&nbsp;have around the world. Unfortunately, the world’s&nbsp;become a&nbsp;much more dangerous place. I&nbsp;think we&nbsp;can’t&nbsp;take for granted anymore democratic values are going to&nbsp;win out—I don’t&nbsp;think that’s&nbsp;clear at&nbsp;all. There are serious&nbsp;threats.</span></p><p><span>So&nbsp;I think we&nbsp;need to&nbsp;work with governments. And also working with governments allows us&nbsp;to work with other regulated important industries too, like banking, health care and so&nbsp;on. Nothing’s&nbsp;changed about our principles. The fundamental thing about our principles has always been: we’ve&nbsp;got to&nbsp;thoughtfully weigh up&nbsp;the benefits, and they’ve&nbsp;got to&nbsp;substantially outweigh the risk of&nbsp;harm. So&nbsp;that’s&nbsp;a high bar for anything that we&nbsp;might want to&nbsp;do. Of&nbsp;course, we’ve&nbsp;got to&nbsp;respect international law and human rights—that’s&nbsp;all still in&nbsp;there.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>In&nbsp;particular, he&nbsp;says:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>The fundamental thing about our principles has always been: we’ve&nbsp;got to&nbsp;thoughtfully weigh up&nbsp;the benefits, and they’ve&nbsp;got to&nbsp;substantially outweigh the risk of&nbsp;harm.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>That’s&nbsp;not a&nbsp;principle. A&nbsp;principle is&nbsp;something you commit to&nbsp;in advance so&nbsp;that you can’t&nbsp;talk yourself out of&nbsp;it later, </span><i><span>even when</span></i><span> the benefits seem to&nbsp;outweigh the harms. One cannot violate a&nbsp;“principle” of&nbsp;“I’ll&nbsp;decide when I see&nbsp;it.”</span></p><p><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20180620101825/https://ai.google/principles/"><span>Google’s&nbsp;original 2018 AI&nbsp;principles</span></a><span> committed that Google would not support specific use cases, leading to&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.businessinsider.com/google-drops-out-of-10-billion-jedi-contract-bid-2018-10"><span>Google dropping its bid for a&nbsp;$10 billion contract in&nbsp;2018</span></a><span>. The principles included a&nbsp;section titled “Applications we&nbsp;will not pursue,” which said that Google would not design or&nbsp;deploy AI&nbsp;for weapons “whose principal purpose or&nbsp;implementation is&nbsp;to cause or&nbsp;directly facilitate injury to&nbsp;people,” nor for surveillance “violating internationally accepted&nbsp;norms.”</span></p><p><span>On&nbsp;February 4th, 2025, Demis co-authored </span><a href="https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/products/responsible-ai-2024-report-ongoing-work/"><span>a post</span></a><span> announcing updates to&nbsp;those principles. </span><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20260430015915/https://ai.google/principles/"><span>The updated principles</span></a><span> removed the prohibitions on&nbsp;weapons and surveillance.</span></p><p><span>Consider these statements: “Demis removed the prohibitions from Google’s&nbsp;AI principles” and “nothing’s&nbsp;changed about our principles.” Both cannot be&nbsp;true.</span></p><p><span>Demis wanted GDM employees to&nbsp;trust him and to&nbsp;trust that DeepMind has a&nbsp;sufficiently strong review process. But Google’s&nbsp;AI principles named things Google would not do, and then he&nbsp;removed those prohibitions, and finally told us&nbsp;nothing had&nbsp;changed.</span></p><p><span>On&nbsp;my last day, I&nbsp;pointed out this discrepancy in&nbsp;the discussion channel. Many GDM employees expressed their disappointment.</span></p><h2><span>We&nbsp;can “work with Western democracies” to&nbsp;“beat China” without giving in&nbsp;to every demand Trump&nbsp;makes</span></h2><p><i><span>When an&nbsp;AI leader says they need to&nbsp;“work with Western democracies,” that’s&nbsp;a hint they’re&nbsp;doing something&nbsp;bad.</span></i></p><p><span>Demis justified breaking GDM’s&nbsp;no-weapons commitment by&nbsp;saying “[Google needs] to&nbsp;work with governments” to&nbsp;ensure that democratic values win out. I&nbsp;think that imposes a&nbsp;false dichotomy. To&nbsp;see why, grant the whole worldview: that if&nbsp;the US&nbsp;doesn’t&nbsp;adopt autonomous weapons, we&nbsp;lose the world to&nbsp;authoritarianism—Ukraine falls, liberty crumbles, and a&nbsp;red wave consumes. Even </span><i><span>with</span></i><span> that strong assumption, “give the Pentagon what it&nbsp;demands” isn’t&nbsp;necessarily the best action for the world. You can, say, construct </span><a href="https://turntrout.com/military-ai"><span>a governance framework</span></a><span> restricting which use cases Google is&nbsp;willing to&nbsp;provide for its products. That would let Google provide the ethical uses (for “beating China”) without the unethical&nbsp;ones.</span></p><p><span>Even if&nbsp;Google </span><i><span>had</span></i><span> been forced to&nbsp;sign, it&nbsp;could still spend its enormous influence lobbying Congress for legal safeguards. Capitulation was a&nbsp;choice.</span></p><h2><span>Building a&nbsp;world-reshaping technology on personal&nbsp;trust</span></h2><p><span>I&nbsp;used to&nbsp;think of&nbsp;Demis as&nbsp;a quiet, thoughtful guy doing the best he&nbsp;can in&nbsp;a demanding corporate structure. Again I&nbsp;return to&nbsp;the story of&nbsp;Demis trying (and failing) to&nbsp;split off Google&nbsp;DeepMind:</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://colossus.com/article/project-mario-demis-hassabis-deepmind-mallaby/"><b><span>The Infinity Machine: Demis Hassabis, DeepMind, and the Quest for Superintelligence</span></b></a></p><p><span>“When we&nbsp;were negotiating with Google, we&nbsp;wanted to&nbsp;ensure safety in&nbsp;a way that would be&nbsp;trustless,” Hassabis said. “That’s&nbsp;actually very difficult to&nbsp;do in&nbsp;reality.</span></p><p><span>“Safety isn’t&nbsp;about governance structures,” he&nbsp;went on. “I&nbsp;mean, even if&nbsp;you have a&nbsp;governance board, it&nbsp;probably wouldn’t&nbsp;do the right thing when it&nbsp;came to the&nbsp;crunch.</span></p><p><span>“Same thing with a&nbsp;safety charter. You can try to&nbsp;negotiate one. But it’s&nbsp;not realistic to&nbsp;create bright‑line principles years in&nbsp;advance because you’ll&nbsp;probably draw the lines in&nbsp;the wrong places. […]</span></p><p><span>“So&nbsp;then I&nbsp;thought, why don’t&nbsp;I go&nbsp;the other way? Take the energy that was going into the trustless negotiation and put it&nbsp;into creating real trust—trust that was actually useful. Try leaning into Google rather than leaning&nbsp;out.</span></p><p><span>“And then of&nbsp;course two things happen. First, you are now at&nbsp;the table, so&nbsp;when a&nbsp;safety issue comes up, you can help to&nbsp;decide it. Second, you get to&nbsp;know the Google people and you rack up&nbsp;successes together. You can’t&nbsp;just talk about trust. You have to earn&nbsp;it.</span></p><p><span>“And I&nbsp;think for me, and maybe for Mustafa, too, it’s&nbsp;about us&nbsp;growing up,” Hassabis mused. “We&nbsp;went through those negotiations and we&nbsp;matured. Things aren’t&nbsp;black and white, especially when you are dealing with a&nbsp;technology with unknown consequences.</span></p><p><span>“So&nbsp;you have to&nbsp;be adaptable. You have to&nbsp;move from idealist to&nbsp;realist, but hopefully still with your&nbsp;values.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Demis is&nbsp;wary of&nbsp;trustless structures. I&nbsp;think that’s&nbsp;backwards: you should try to&nbsp;lower how much trust a&nbsp;system requires. The </span><a href="https://turntrout.com/military-ai"><span>Framework</span></a><span> I&nbsp;proposed rests on&nbsp;a single trust assumption: that the Chief Scientist is&nbsp;reasonable long enough to&nbsp;seat the Review Body. The Framework then manufactures justified trust through transparency and contract, not </span><i><span>preventing</span></i><span> unethical deals but adding friction. Demis’s&nbsp;objection is&nbsp;that a&nbsp;governance board “probably wouldn’t&nbsp;do the right thing when it&nbsp;came to&nbsp;the crunch.” True, but </span><i><span>a person at&nbsp;the table is&nbsp;subject to&nbsp;exactly the same crunch but with no&nbsp;transparency and with worse incentives: equity, social bonds with colleagues, and a&nbsp;self-image tied to&nbsp;the company</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>And notice how Demis arrived at&nbsp;his philosophy. He&nbsp;tried to&nbsp;build a&nbsp;lower-trust structure by&nbsp;spinning DeepMind out from Google under a&nbsp;semi-independent board. Sundar refused. Only then did Demis conclude that the answer was mutual trust and a&nbsp;seat at the&nbsp;table.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Try leaning into Google rather than leaning&nbsp;out.</span></p><p><span>And then of&nbsp;course two things happen. First, you are now at&nbsp;the table, so&nbsp;when a&nbsp;safety issue comes up, you can help to&nbsp;decide it. Second, you get to&nbsp;know the Google people and you rack up&nbsp;successes together. You can’t&nbsp;just talk about trust. You have to earn&nbsp;it.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>So&nbsp;what has his seat produced? Demis has been at&nbsp;the table for every contract in&nbsp;this essay. The classified deal made zero binding concessions to&nbsp;the employees raising ethical concerns. Maybe his presence averted something worse, but Google’s&nbsp;terms were near the floor of&nbsp;what I&nbsp;imagined possible. The ethical terms are non-binding and therefore weaker than OpenAI’s. If&nbsp;his seat were worth what he&nbsp;says, you’d&nbsp;expect more to&nbsp;show for it.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwkr6qrnb2gj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwkr6qrnb2gj">[16]</a></sup></span></p><h1><span>Reflections</span></h1><p><span>Google DeepMind was an&nbsp;</span><i><span>experiment</span></i><span> in&nbsp;governance. The cofounders sold to&nbsp;Google on&nbsp;a </span><a href="https://time.com/7013685/google-ai-deepmind-military-contracts-israel/"><span>promise never to&nbsp;power weapons</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://colossus.com/article/project-mario-demis-hassabis-deepmind-mallaby/"><span>fought for a&nbsp;semi-independent governance structure</span></a><span>. Sundar refused it. Google’s&nbsp;2018 AI&nbsp;Principles were imposed by&nbsp;employee pressure but later </span><a href="https://www.cnbc.com/2025/02/04/google-removes-pledge-to-not-use-ai-for-weapons-surveillance.html"><span>quietly defanged by&nbsp;leadership</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Here’s&nbsp;the result of&nbsp;GDM’s&nbsp;experiment: it&nbsp;failed.</span></p><p><span>When profit and pressure met ethical commitment at&nbsp;Google DeepMind, pressure won and pledges lost. When profit and pressure met ethical commitment at&nbsp;Anthropic, ethics won. So&nbsp;the lesson is&nbsp;not “no&nbsp;one ever takes a&nbsp;stand.” The lesson is&nbsp;that society cannot </span><i><span>rely</span></i><span> on&nbsp;ethics-motivated people standing&nbsp;firm.</span></p><p><span>I&nbsp;know the other options don’t&nbsp;look great. Congress remains a&nbsp;potted plant in&nbsp;the corner. But we&nbsp;should at&nbsp;least stop </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Building_a_world_reshaping_technology_on_personal_trust" rel="noreferrer"><span>telling ourselves that a&nbsp;seat at&nbsp;the table works</span></a><span>.</span></p><h2><span>How can a&nbsp;pledge-signer remain at&nbsp;GDM?</span></h2><p><span>Jeff Dean, Demis Hassabis, Shane Legg, and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#preparing-for-lunch-with-jeff-dean"><span>other senior employees</span></a><span> pledged to&nbsp;“neither participate in&nbsp;nor support the development, manufacture, trade, or&nbsp;use of&nbsp;lethal autonomous weapons.” Google signed the classified deal, yet they&nbsp;remain.</span></p><p><span>One might defend: “the deal simply doesn’t&nbsp;</span><i><span>prohibit</span></i><span> autonomous weapons, that’s&nbsp;not the same as&nbsp;actively supporting autonomous weapons.” Google’s&nbsp;contract withholds certainty from a&nbsp;concerned pledge-signer, but a&nbsp;blindfold does not absolve responsibility.</span></p><p><span>Your company is&nbsp;trying to&nbsp;make and then supply the best AI&nbsp;in the world to&nbsp;a military which wants</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref92pehg9cqm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn92pehg9cqm">[17]</a></sup></span><span> to&nbsp;use AI&nbsp;in lethal autonomous weapons. Your company signed away its ability to&nbsp;restrict use cases. You chose to&nbsp;stay and continue building that&nbsp;AI.</span></p><p><span>That looks a&nbsp;damn lot like “support the development of&nbsp;lethal autonomous weapons” to&nbsp;me.</span></p><p><span>What should a&nbsp;pledge-signer do? I&nbsp;see three honest options: explain publicly how staying is&nbsp;consistent with the pledge, say plainly that you no&nbsp;longer hold it&nbsp;and why, or&nbsp;quit. Wearing the pledge while saying nothing isn’t&nbsp;one of&nbsp;them.</span></p><h3><span>Keeping a&nbsp;seat at the&nbsp;table</span></h3><p><span>But should GDM employees not stay to&nbsp;keep steering in&nbsp;a positive direction? To&nbsp;this I&nbsp;must object: “What steering?”. This deal may have been the clearest red line Google’s&nbsp;Gemini project will ever face, and yet the deal came out with no&nbsp;concessions to&nbsp;ethics-concerned employees. If&nbsp;their “seat at&nbsp;the table” couldn’t&nbsp;produce a&nbsp;single binding provision in&nbsp;</span><i><span>that</span></i><span> situation, then when would&nbsp;it?</span></p><p><span>Plus, a&nbsp;pledge is&nbsp;only worth the credibility behind it. When someone signs “I&nbsp;will not support the development of&nbsp;lethal autonomous weapons,” then </span><i><span>stays</span></i><span> while their company sells unrestricted AI&nbsp;to a&nbsp;military that wants exactly that, they teach every counterparty a&nbsp;lesson: these safety people will not act, even at&nbsp;their own brightest line. The next commitment they make is&nbsp;worth less. Eventually it’s worth&nbsp;nothing.</span></p><h2><span>The weight of&nbsp;ethics</span></h2><p><span>Where lies the blame? Pete Hegseth and Donald Trump, who intimidated the AI&nbsp;companies? Sundar Pichai, who signed the deal? Jeff Dean, whose leverage left no&nbsp;mark on&nbsp;the deal? The responsibility splits over them unevenly, but my&nbsp;attention rests on&nbsp;those with stated ethical commitments.</span></p><p><span>With Pete Hegseth, you at&nbsp;least know what you’re&nbsp;getting. “Persuade Pete to&nbsp;stop </span><a href="https://www.militarytimes.com/news/your-military/2025/12/01/former-jags-say-hegseth-others-may-have-committed-war-crimes/"><span>ordering</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.rawstory.com/hegseth-2676100354/"><span>war crimes</span></a><span>” is&nbsp;not an&nbsp;available strategy. But I&nbsp;feel uniquely disappointed in&nbsp;the consistent inaction of&nbsp;nearly all of&nbsp;these senior AI&nbsp;professionals who talk about&nbsp;ethics.</span></p><p><span>Why did they choose inaction? The answer does not seem clean and simple, but I&nbsp;think part of&nbsp;the answer is&nbsp;fear of&nbsp;the Trump administration.</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://www.cbsnews.com/news/godfather-of-ai-geoffrey-hinton-ai-warning/"><b><span>A&nbsp;long-form interview with Geoffrey Hinton</span></b></a><span> (April 26th, 2025)</span></p><p><b><span>Interviewer:</span></b><span> Were you disappointed when Google went back on&nbsp;its promise not to&nbsp;use military&nbsp;AI?</span></p><p><b><span>Hinton:</span></b><span> Very disappointed. Particularly since I&nbsp;knew Sergey Brin didn’t&nbsp;like military use of&nbsp;AI.</span></p><p><b><span>Interviewer:</span></b><span> Why do&nbsp;you think they did&nbsp;it?</span></p><p><b><span>Hinton:</span></b><span> I&nbsp;don’t&nbsp;have any inside information… I&nbsp;could speculate that they were worried about being ill-treated by&nbsp;the current administration if&nbsp;they wouldn’t&nbsp;make weapons for the&nbsp;US.</span></p></blockquote><h2><span>What are the AI luminaries&nbsp;doing?</span></h2><p><span>These people are clearly not cowards in&nbsp;general. For years, Stuart staked out the unpopular position that AI&nbsp;existential risk should be&nbsp;taken seriously. Hinton left the US&nbsp;in the 1980s </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Hinton#Politics"><span>due to&nbsp;his “disapproval of&nbsp;military funding of&nbsp;artificial intelligence”</span></a><span> and then left Google in&nbsp;2023 to&nbsp;speak frankly about the risks of&nbsp;AI.</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://www.msn.com/en-us/technology/artificial-intelligence/godfather-of-ai-geoffrey-hinton-says-the-war-in-ukraine-changed-his-view-of-military-ai/ar-AA25hdtV"><b><span>Geoffrey Hinton</span></b></a><span> (June 10, 2026)</span></p><p><span>The only thing that’s&nbsp;going to&nbsp;rein in&nbsp;those big AI&nbsp;companies is public&nbsp;pressure.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>So&nbsp;where was the&nbsp;pressure?</span></p><p><span>Sometimes a&nbsp;person will ask, “what if&nbsp;IASEAI was just saving up&nbsp;political capital for an&nbsp;even more impactful moment?”. One answer is&nbsp;that “saving capital” can’t&nbsp;explain why IASEAI ignored low-cost opportunities (like Stuart connecting me&nbsp;with key Google decision-makers).</span></p><p><span>What I’d&nbsp;expect, given Stuart’s&nbsp;career, is&nbsp;for him to&nbsp;speak out. Stuart gave hundreds of&nbsp;talks (to&nbsp;the UN, to&nbsp;the Senate, to&nbsp;an interviewer) railing against slaughterbots. He&nbsp;</span><a href="https://spectrum.ieee.org/why-you-should-fear-slaughterbots-a-response"><span>thundered against “wishful thinking” in&nbsp;the pages of&nbsp;IEEE Spectrum.</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnfei9wx9kq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnfei9wx9kq">[18]</a></sup></span><span> He&nbsp;made strong statements </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Stuart_closes_out_IASEAI" rel="noreferrer"><span>onstage at&nbsp;IASEAI closing</span></a><span>. But faced with a&nbsp;real chance to&nbsp;speak out against a&nbsp;</span><i><span>specific powerful adversary</span></i><span>, he fell&nbsp;silent.</span></p><h2><span>Why didn’t&nbsp;Jeff put his foot&nbsp;down?</span></h2><p><span>Jeff’s&nbsp;a tough case. Out of&nbsp;all Google’s&nbsp;executives, he&nbsp;was the only one to&nbsp;act publicly. He&nbsp;signed the amicus and broke from his company. That probably made things awkward within the C-suite. I respect&nbsp;that.</span></p><p><span>He&nbsp;had the power to&nbsp;do more. And I&nbsp;really wish he&nbsp;had. I&nbsp;think he&nbsp;could have stopped the deal, yet he&nbsp;did not. He&nbsp;remains, yet I&nbsp;think he should&nbsp;not.</span></p><h2><span>Breaking free of&nbsp;roles</span></h2><p><span>I&nbsp;was a&nbsp;research scientist, you know—one of&nbsp;hundreds at&nbsp;GDM.&nbsp;I have a&nbsp;picture of&nbsp;a “responsible” research scientist in&nbsp;my head. The “responsible” research scientist makes a&nbsp;tweet and then sends their manager a&nbsp;concerned message about ICE contracts. The “responsible” research scientist doesn’t&nbsp;cold-message Google executives.</span></p><p><span>Rarely, a&nbsp;person will </span><i><span>break their bounds</span></i><span>. They step outside of&nbsp;the fear which held them. They admit a&nbsp;difficult truth. They act in&nbsp;a way that would have surprised them the day before. I&nbsp;do not understand why people do&nbsp;or don’t&nbsp;break their&nbsp;bounds.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 37.77001602772139%;"><img alt="A framed photo of Alex Pretti, a man with a beard and glasses, adorned with wooden rosary beads. Below the frame, a handwritten note on cardboard reads, &quot;Rest in Peace ALEX Pretti.&quot;" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iKm2FhpWkuuBojm82/wh6iwy0k0rnb4malecvz" /></figure><p><span>I&nbsp;know what broke my&nbsp;bounds in&nbsp;this instance. I&nbsp;have a&nbsp;regular reminder in&nbsp;my phone which shows me&nbsp;a picture of&nbsp;Alex Pretti. In&nbsp;one January moment, my&nbsp;anger flared so&nbsp;hot that its </span><i><span>only outlet</span></i><span> was to&nbsp;find a&nbsp;plan which could actually work. A&nbsp;mere tweet would do&nbsp;nothing and would count for nothing. Only a&nbsp;good plan would satiate. The anger </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotspot_(geology)"><span>burned through my&nbsp;bounds</span></a><span> and broke&nbsp;them.</span></p><p><span>When I&nbsp;got scared—and I&nbsp;did—I’d&nbsp;think about Minneapolis. I’d&nbsp;think about ICE </span><a href="https://www.thetrace.org/2025/12/immigration-ice-shootings-guns-tracker/"><span>shooting people in&nbsp;the street</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://courthousenews.com/minnesotans-sue-feds-over-warrantless-ice-raids/"><span>dragging people from their homes</span></a><span>.</span></p><h1><span>Why I&nbsp;left Google&nbsp;DeepMind</span></h1><p><span>When an employee leaves a top AI lab, it's often into the arms of another. They usually rack up a huge bonus that way. That's not what I did: I didn't flirt with competitor labs and I declined outreach from the OpenAI safety team. I'm unemployed right now.</span></p><p><span>In&nbsp;February, I&nbsp;realized that Google would probably sign the deal, which made me&nbsp;think about the door. I&nbsp;realized maybe I&nbsp;should leave and maybe I&nbsp;could do&nbsp;better AI&nbsp;safety work elsewhere. But I&nbsp;think I&nbsp;would have stayed a&nbsp;few more months if&nbsp;they hadn’t&nbsp;signed the deal. When Google signed, I&nbsp;just couldn’t&nbsp;do any more work. My&nbsp;brain said&nbsp;“no.”</span></p><p><span>When I&nbsp;next went to&nbsp;the office, the building felt like a&nbsp;memory. Like going home to&nbsp;your old high school: it&nbsp;used to&nbsp;be the center of&nbsp;your hopes and dreams, and then one day you just know that you don’t&nbsp;belong there&nbsp;anymore.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="The view from an office window overlooking the San Francisco–Oakland Bay Bridge, showing the blue water of the bay, a palm tree in the foreground, and a coastal road with traffic below. A small section of rainbow peeks above Treasure Island." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iKm2FhpWkuuBojm82/casa9xoe8xnzniofycg1" /><figcaption><p><br /><span>The view from my&nbsp;desk at&nbsp;GDM.&nbsp;March 2024.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><h1><span>Appendix: Anticipated&nbsp;questions</span></h1><h2><span>What if&nbsp;the people you critique were saving their political&nbsp;capital?</span></h2><p><span>Indeed, it’s&nbsp;not always rational to&nbsp;say what you think the moment you think it. However, “saving political capital” explains avoiding a&nbsp;costly public statement. It&nbsp;doesn’t&nbsp;explain refusing costless private help. I&nbsp;asked IASEAI for a&nbsp;private introduction and got&nbsp;nothing.</span></p><h2><span>Maybe they thought you weren’t&nbsp;worth their time; you aren’t&nbsp;entitled to their&nbsp;help</span></h2><p><span>Absolutely. No&nbsp;one should be&nbsp;castigated simply because they didn’t&nbsp;follow my&nbsp;particular recommendations. But what I&nbsp;would expect to&nbsp;see is&nbsp;any kind of&nbsp;action at&nbsp;all.</span></p><p><span>More broadly, sympathetic stories predict visible impact, including “visible in&nbsp;its consequences.” That’s&nbsp;part of&nbsp;why I’m&nbsp;comfortable guessing that Jeff did not put his foot down and threaten to&nbsp;walk. If&nbsp;he </span><i><span>had</span></i><span> put his foot down, I&nbsp;expect the world would look different to&nbsp;me: in&nbsp;particular, I&nbsp;would expect the classified deal to&nbsp;contain at&nbsp;least </span><i><span>some</span></i><span> binding provisions.</span></p><h2><span>Every person shouldn’t&nbsp;have to&nbsp;speak out about every&nbsp;issue</span></h2><p><span>Yes, but if&nbsp;you promise to&nbsp;take an&nbsp;action and then don’t, that’s&nbsp;different. IASEAI promised to&nbsp;hold a&nbsp;member poll and never did. Further, if&nbsp;you built a&nbsp;significant part of&nbsp;your identity on&nbsp;opposing something,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefeod0p9an5t"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fneod0p9an5t">[19]</a></sup></span><span> I&nbsp;think it’s&nbsp;fair to&nbsp;discuss the decision to&nbsp;stay silent while that thing&nbsp;is decided. Stuart is&nbsp;simply the clearest case. He&nbsp;spent a&nbsp;decade as&nbsp;the loudest voice against autonomous weapons, then went quiet at&nbsp;the first real collision between modern AI&nbsp;and military&nbsp;use.</span></p><h2><span>Even if&nbsp;Google had adopted your Framework, the Pentagon would have&nbsp;refused</span></h2><p><span>I&nbsp;agree. xAI would still have given over their AI.&nbsp;But if&nbsp;Google had given signs of&nbsp;independence earlier, it&nbsp;could perhaps have built a&nbsp;coalition with OpenAI and&nbsp;Anthropic.</span></p><p><span>Consider also that Anthropic taking a&nbsp;stand is&nbsp;one tech company. If&nbsp;a company like Google also defied the administration, I&nbsp;think that would have transformed the tech industry’s&nbsp;meekness into independence. Instead…</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://www.nextgov.com/artificial-intelligence/2026/05/pentagon-will-never-again-rely-single-ai-provider-official-says/413399/"><b><span>Pentagon will ‘never again’ rely on&nbsp;a single AI&nbsp;provider, official&nbsp;says</span></b></a></p><p><span>Defense Under Secretary for Research and Engineering Emil Michael said new agreements with Big Tech companies are a&nbsp;“counterstatement” to&nbsp;the ongoing Anthropic-Pentagon conflict as&nbsp;the agency prioritizes flexible contracts. […]</span></p><p><span>Michael continued to&nbsp;say that the new deals with Amazon Web Services, Google, Microsoft, NVIDIA, OpenAI, Reflection, Oracle and SpaceX are “a&nbsp;statement by&nbsp;the biggest tech companies in&nbsp;the world who are involved in&nbsp;the AI&nbsp;space … and have them say, ‘We&nbsp;support the Department of&nbsp;War, we&nbsp;support the U.S. government, and we&nbsp;support the… armed services for all lawful use&nbsp;cases.’”</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Despite the Pentagon’s&nbsp;policy, Jeff’s&nbsp;leverage mattered. The Pentagon would have refused, yes, but then Google could have walked&nbsp;away.</span></p><h2><span>Does this have any impact on&nbsp;existential risk from&nbsp;AI?</span></h2><p><span>Yes.</span></p><p><span>When building an&nbsp;advanced AI&nbsp;system, best practice is&nbsp;to make a&nbsp;“safety case” which explains why the system will be&nbsp;aligned and will not cause catastrophic harm. I&nbsp;think any credible GDM safety case would lean heavily on&nbsp;monitoring the “chain of&nbsp;thought,” a&nbsp;mechanism their </span><a href="https://storage.googleapis.com/deepmind-media/DeepMind.com/Blog/strengthening-our-frontier-safety-framework/frontier-safety-framework_3-1.pdf"><span>Frontier Safety Framework</span></a><span> discusses.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefh0ku4j3al5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnh0ku4j3al5">[20]</a></sup></span><span> For the unfamiliar, a&nbsp;chain of&nbsp;thought is&nbsp;the AI&nbsp;roughly explaining what it’s&nbsp;doing and why. It’s&nbsp;not perfectly accurate, but it’s&nbsp;extremely informative.</span></p><p><span>An&nbsp;AI that wants to&nbsp;hurt us&nbsp;won’t&nbsp;announce it&nbsp;to our faces because we&nbsp;would shut it&nbsp;off and then it&nbsp;couldn’t&nbsp;achieve its (misaligned) goals. So&nbsp;the AI&nbsp;will likely be&nbsp;deceptive. One of&nbsp;the best ways we&nbsp;can detect deception is&nbsp;by looking at&nbsp;the chain of&nbsp;thought. To&nbsp;look at&nbsp;the chain of&nbsp;thought, there must be&nbsp;trained human overseers who can access and analyze the data. But no&nbsp;one </span><i><span>can</span></i><span> do&nbsp;that: Google is&nbsp;handing over its AI&nbsp;to run in&nbsp;a secured military data center that, by&nbsp;default, won’t&nbsp;have trained overseers performing this analysis, and that data center obviously isn’t&nbsp;transmitting data back to&nbsp;Google!</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6int65dkpwc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6int65dkpwc">[21]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>I’m&nbsp;</span><i><span>not</span></i><span> saying that Google engineers should read what the military is&nbsp;doing. I’m&nbsp;saying that by&nbsp;default, there won’t&nbsp;be appropriately trained military engineers who will perform this monitoring. If&nbsp;an AI&nbsp;is not monitored for deception in&nbsp;its chain of&nbsp;thought, it&nbsp;will have an&nbsp;easier time causing catastrophic damage to&nbsp;humanity by&nbsp;scheming, deceiving, and trying to&nbsp;take over. That’s&nbsp;bad.</span></p><p><span>Unfortunately, that’s&nbsp;only half the problem! A&nbsp;military deployment setting without chain of&nbsp;thought deception monitoring would be&nbsp;a juicy target for a&nbsp;rogue AI, offering both weak oversight of&nbsp;scheming and access to&nbsp;powerful decision-makers and infrastructure.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnmj69tlyc6f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnmj69tlyc6f">[22]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Hopefully, the military (in&nbsp;conjunction with the </span><a href="https://www.nist.gov/caisi"><span>US CAISI</span></a><span>) develops expertise, caution, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.06942"><span>control procedures</span></a><span> for monitoring and containing rogue AI&nbsp;systems.</span></p><h1><span>Appendix: “Don’t&nbsp;worry, it’s&nbsp;only API&nbsp;access”</span></h1><p><span>A&nbsp;common reassurance from management:</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://www.reuters.com/technology/google-signs-classified-ai-deal-with-pentagon-information-reports-2026-04-28/"><b><span>A&nbsp;Google spokesperson</span></b></a></p><p><span>We&nbsp;believe that providing API access to&nbsp;our commercial models, including on&nbsp;Google infrastructure, with industry-standard practices and terms, represents a&nbsp;responsible approach to&nbsp;supporting national&nbsp;security.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>“API access” is&nbsp;misleading. When you or&nbsp;I think of&nbsp;“API access,” we&nbsp;think of&nbsp;sending requests to&nbsp;the AI&nbsp;provider, which can then scan the requests and ensure the uses are acceptable.</span></p><p><span>Imagine a&nbsp;commander on&nbsp;a mission consulting with Gemini. Do&nbsp;you think he’s&nbsp;sending plaintext queries to&nbsp;Google, where Google could (theoretically) read up&nbsp;on the classified mission details? No. That’d be&nbsp;crazy.</span></p><p><span>Instead, my&nbsp;guess is&nbsp;that Google runs </span><i><span>on-premise API access.</span></i><span> Here’s&nbsp;the story that makes sense to&nbsp;me: the government has a&nbsp;secure Cloud computing cluster with no&nbsp;connection back to&nbsp;Google’s&nbsp;server farms. Google drops off one or&nbsp;more servers which expose a&nbsp;Gemini API endpoint to&nbsp;the military’s&nbsp;cluster.</span></p><p><span>“API access only,” then, would be&nbsp;technically true but misleading (wrongly suggests centralized supervision by&nbsp;Google) and irrelevant. What protection does API access provide? The problem is&nbsp;using Gemini to&nbsp;assist in&nbsp;potential war crimes and mass profiling of&nbsp;dissidents. Thanks in&nbsp;part to&nbsp;Google, however, these terms are now “industry-standard practice” (outside of&nbsp;Anthropic).</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnumab785l62"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefumab785l62">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Technically, I’m&nbsp;worried about mass </span><i><span>profiling</span></i><span> from AI.&nbsp;Surveillance concerns data collection. Profiling takes data and draws conclusions, like “does this person dislike the government?”.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlqhifp04p09"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflqhifp04p09">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Google replies that immigration agents are merely </span><a href="https://subscriber.politicopro.com/article/2026/02/google-employees-call-on-tech-giant-to-end-homeland-security-contracts-00768991"><span>using commercially available cloud services</span></a><span>. But the problem was never that Google provided </span><i><span>special</span></i><span> services; it’s&nbsp;that Google provided services to&nbsp;ICE at&nbsp;all.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnf992iatjv3c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreff992iatjv3c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The ToS’s&nbsp;exceptions didn’t&nbsp;apply in&nbsp;the case of&nbsp;the student&nbsp;protester.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn12dy8r7fixde"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref12dy8r7fixde">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Judge Lin </span><a href="https://storage.courtlistener.com/recap/gov.uscourts.cand.465515/gov.uscourts.cand.465515.134.0.pdf"><span>later said</span></a><span> that the Pentagon’s&nbsp;supply chain risk designation was “classic illegal First Amendment retaliation.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fny0eb4erto2"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefy0eb4erto2">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Stuart’s&nbsp;“extortion racket” comments seemingly only featured incidentally in&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.theinformation.com/newsletters/ai-agenda/robot-data-startup-raises-60-million"><span>one news round-up by&nbsp;The Information</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpgwbmey1oes"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpgwbmey1oes">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>On&nbsp;that Thursday in&nbsp;the same venue building, a&nbsp;</span><a href="https://thefuturesociety.org/ai-red-lines-iaseai-workshop/"><span>four-hour workshop</span></a><span> convened on&nbsp;how to&nbsp;make AI&nbsp;red lines “verifiable and enforceable.” Participants were asked: what is&nbsp;the biggest obstacle to&nbsp;these red lines? </span><a href="https://thefuturesociety.org/ai-red-lines-iaseai-workshop/"><span>Political will</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntv1k0vi2hn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftv1k0vi2hn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Upon request, Mark </span><i><span>did</span></i><span> refund my membership&nbsp;dues.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnf52bc9fabga"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreff52bc9fabga">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In&nbsp;my GDM discussion message, I&nbsp;originally estimated “killing over 150 people.” As&nbsp;of June 28th, 2026, the toll has </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/interactive/2025/10/29/us/us-caribbean-pacific-boat-strikes.html"><span>risen to&nbsp;215</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn05qoyf4mctsi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref05qoyf4mctsi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Throughout my&nbsp;internal campaign, I&nbsp;made sure not to&nbsp;talk to&nbsp;the media (even off the record). I&nbsp;wanted change from the inside and I&nbsp;wanted to&nbsp;do it&nbsp;right by&nbsp;people like&nbsp;Jeff.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndg4y9oqdsau"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdg4y9oqdsau">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The New York Times also reported:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>A&nbsp;footnote in&nbsp;the A.I. letter to&nbsp;Mr.&nbsp;Dean said many of&nbsp;the signees opposed “warrantless surveillance of&nbsp;any citizens of&nbsp;the world.” But they decided to&nbsp;exclude that from the letter “to&nbsp;increase the probability of&nbsp;achieving our&nbsp;request.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span>I regret that decision. "Americans only" protections lined up with Anthropic's red lines, but "Americans only" was too small of an ask. Don't negotiate away what you want before the negotiations even begin! My </span><a href="https://turntrout.com/military-ai" rel="noreferrer"><span>proposed contract language</span></a><span> later demanded more protection for non-Americans.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndrzs9i6nfno"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdrzs9i6nfno">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I&nbsp;was responsible for 8 of&nbsp;the 18 GDM signatures on&nbsp;the amicus brief. The count was low because it was&nbsp;impromptu.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnotvsgfp6jrs"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefotvsgfp6jrs">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is&nbsp;why I&nbsp;think my&nbsp;actions led to&nbsp;additional Pentagon wariness. I&nbsp;introduced Jeff (and nearly half of&nbsp;the Google signers) to&nbsp;the amicus brief. They signed. The Pentagon hesitated in&nbsp;part due to&nbsp;the Google signatures on&nbsp;the amicus&nbsp;brief.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnunv7cpolnm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefunv7cpolnm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Large coalitions have problems here. Imagine my&nbsp;plan relied on&nbsp;several senior GDM employees telling Sundar they’ll&nbsp;walk if&nbsp;the deal gets signed. Sundar doesn’t&nbsp;need to&nbsp;talk them all down; he&nbsp;would just need to&nbsp;fracture their intent to&nbsp;act. Offer the more senior employees a&nbsp;$15 million retention bonus. If&nbsp;a few bite, the coalition crumbles. No&nbsp;one knows how on&nbsp;board the others really are, and they don’t&nbsp;think they individually can bring about change by&nbsp;quitting, and so&nbsp;they don’t&nbsp;quit.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6i1k26tf8k"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6i1k26tf8k">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The government’s&nbsp;July 8th deadline was another reason that “Google will just wait it&nbsp;out” struck me&nbsp;as implausible.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlrvmfqx4duf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflrvmfqx4duf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I&nbsp;didn’t&nbsp;think that “no&nbsp;classified contracts” was the right line to&nbsp;draw, but I&nbsp;thought it&nbsp;was better than no&nbsp;line ### [Monthly Roundup #44: July 2026](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KiCwcAGHx4rdwJgzD/monthly-roundup-44-july-2026)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>It’s a quiet week so let’s do the monthly right on schedule.</p>
